Original: WITH all_sales AS (  SELECT d_year        ,i_brand_id        ,i_class_id        ,i_category_id        ,i_manufact_id        ,SUM(sales_cnt) AS sales_cnt        ,SUM(sales_amt) AS sales_amt  FROM (SELECT d_year              ,i_brand_id              ,i_class_id              ,i_category_id              ,i_manufact_id              ,cs_quantity - COALESCE(cr_return_quantity,0) AS sales_cnt              ,cs_ext_sales_price - COALESCE(cr_return_amount,0.0) AS sales_amt        FROM catalog_sales JOIN item ON i_item_sk=cs_item_sk                           JOIN date_dim ON d_date_sk=cs_sold_date_sk                           LEFT JOIN catalog_returns ON (cs_order_number=cr_order_number                                                     AND cs_item_sk=cr_item_sk)        WHERE i_category='Sports'        and cs_sales_price / cs_list_price BETWEEN 80 * 0.01 AND 100 * 0.01        and cr_reason_sk in (7, 10, 12, 29, 45)        UNION        SELECT d_year              ,i_brand_id              ,i_class_id              ,i_category_id              ,i_manufact_id              ,ss_quantity - COALESCE(sr_return_quantity,0) AS sales_cnt              ,ss_ext_sales_price - COALESCE(sr_return_amt,0.0) AS sales_amt        FROM store_sales JOIN item ON i_item_sk=ss_item_sk                         JOIN date_dim ON d_date_sk=ss_sold_date_sk                         LEFT JOIN store_returns ON (ss_ticket_number=sr_ticket_number                                                 AND ss_item_sk=sr_item_sk)        WHERE i_category='Sports'        and ss_sales_price / ss_list_price BETWEEN 80 * 0.01 AND 100 * 0.01        and sr_reason_sk in (7, 10, 12, 29, 45)        UNION        SELECT d_year              ,i_brand_id              ,i_class_id              ,i_category_id              ,i_manufact_id              ,ws_quantity - COALESCE(wr_return_quantity,0) AS sales_cnt              ,ws_ext_sales_price - COALESCE(wr_return_amt,0.0) AS sales_amt        FROM web_sales JOIN item ON i_item_sk=ws_item_sk                       JOIN date_dim ON d_date_sk=ws_sold_date_sk                       LEFT JOIN web_returns ON (ws_order_number=wr_order_number                                             AND ws_item_sk=wr_item_sk)        WHERE i_category='Sports'        and ws_sales_price / ws_list_price BETWEEN 80 * 0.01 AND 100 * 0.01        and wr_reason_sk in (7, 10, 12, 29, 45)) sales_detail GROUP BY d_year, i_brand_id, i_class_id, i_category_id, i_manufact_id)  SELECT  prev_yr.d_year AS prev_year                           ,curr_yr.d_year AS year                           ,curr_yr.i_brand_id                           ,curr_yr.i_class_id                           ,curr_yr.i_category_id                           ,curr_yr.i_manufact_id                           ,prev_yr.sales_cnt AS prev_yr_cnt                           ,curr_yr.sales_cnt AS curr_yr_cnt                           ,curr_yr.sales_cnt-prev_yr.sales_cnt AS sales_cnt_diff                           ,curr_yr.sales_amt-prev_yr.sales_amt AS sales_amt_diff  FROM all_sales curr_yr, all_sales prev_yr  WHERE curr_yr.i_brand_id=prev_yr.i_brand_id    AND curr_yr.i_class_id=prev_yr.i_class_id    AND curr_yr.i_category_id=prev_yr.i_category_id    AND curr_yr.i_manufact_id=prev_yr.i_manufact_id    AND curr_yr.d_year=1999    AND prev_yr.d_year=1999-1    AND prev_yr.sales_cnt > 0    AND CAST(curr_yr.sales_cnt AS DECIMAL(17,2))/CAST(prev_yr.sales_cnt AS DECIMAL(17,2))<0.9  ORDER BY sales_cnt_diff,sales_amt_diff  limit 100;
Rewritten: WITH sales_detail AS (
    SELECT
        d_year,
        i_brand_id,
        i_class_id,
        i_category_id,
        i_manufact_id,
        cs_quantity - COALESCE(cr_return_quantity, 0) AS sales_cnt,
        cs_ext_sales_price - COALESCE(cr_return_amount, 0.0) AS sales_amt
    FROM catalog_sales
    JOIN item ON i_item_sk = cs_item_sk
    JOIN date_dim ON d_date_sk = cs_sold_date_sk
    LEFT JOIN catalog_returns
           ON cs_order_number = cr_order_number
          AND cs_item_sk = cr_item_sk
    WHERE i_category = 'Sports'
      AND d_year IN (1998, 1999)
      AND cs_sales_price / cs_list_price BETWEEN 0.80 AND 1.00
      AND cr_reason_sk IN (7, 10, 12, 29, 45)

    UNION

    SELECT
        d_year,
        i_brand_id,
        i_class_id,
        i_category_id,
        i_manufact_id,
        ss_quantity - COALESCE(sr_return_quantity, 0),
        ss_ext_sales_price - COALESCE(sr_return_amt, 0.0)
    FROM store_sales
    JOIN item ON i_item_sk = ss_item_sk
    JOIN date_dim ON d_date_sk = ss_sold_date_sk
    LEFT JOIN store_returns
           ON ss_ticket_number = sr_ticket_number
          AND ss_item_sk = sr_item_sk
    WHERE i_category = 'Sports'
      AND d_year IN (1998, 1999)
      AND ss_sales_price / ss_list_price BETWEEN 0.80 AND 1.00
      AND sr_reason_sk IN (7, 10, 12, 29, 45)

    UNION

    SELECT
        d_year,
        i_brand_id,
        i_class_id,
        i_category_id,
        i_manufact_id,
        ws_quantity - COALESCE(wr_return_quantity, 0),
        ws_ext_sales_price - COALESCE(wr_return_amt, 0.0)
    FROM web_sales
    JOIN item ON i_item_sk = ws_item_sk
    JOIN date_dim ON d_date_sk = ws_sold_date_sk
    LEFT JOIN web_returns
           ON ws_order_number = wr_order_number
          AND ws_item_sk = wr_item_sk
    WHERE i_category = 'Sports'
      AND d_year IN (1998, 1999)
      AND ws_sales_price / ws_list_price BETWEEN 0.80 AND 1.00
      AND wr_reason_sk IN (7, 10, 12, 29, 45)
),
agg AS (
    SELECT
        i_brand_id,
        i_class_id,
        i_category_id,
        i_manufact_id,
        SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1999 THEN sales_cnt END) AS curr_cnt,
        SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1998 THEN sales_cnt END) AS prev_cnt,
        SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1999 THEN sales_amt END) AS curr_amt,
        SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1998 THEN sales_amt END) AS prev_amt
    FROM sales_detail
    GROUP BY i_brand_id, i_class_id, i_category_id, i_manufact_id
)
SELECT
    1998 AS prev_year,
    1999 AS year,
    i_brand_id,
    i_class_id,
    i_category_id,
    i_manufact_id,
    prev_cnt AS prev_yr_cnt,
    curr_cnt AS curr_yr_cnt,
    curr_cnt - prev_cnt AS sales_cnt_diff,
    curr_amt - prev_amt AS sales_amt_diff
FROM agg
WHERE prev_cnt > 0
  AND CAST(curr_cnt AS DECIMAL(17,2)) / CAST(prev_cnt AS DECIMAL(17,2)) < 0.9
ORDER BY sales_cnt_diff, sales_amt_diff
LIMIT 100;

Now, let's consider if we should use `SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1999 THEN sales_cnt END)` or `SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1999 THEN sales_cnt ELSE 0 END)`. The former returns NULL if no rows. We rely on NULL to filter out rows with no current year. This is correct. If we used ELSE 0, we would need an extra count condition. So our choice is correct.

Wait: What if there are rows for 1999 but their `sales_cnt` sum is NULL? That could only happen if all `sales_cnt` are NULL. But `sales_cnt` is `cs_quantity - COALESCE(cr_return_quantity, 0)`. `cs_quantity` is likely NOT NULL. So `sales_cnt` is not null. So `SUM` returns a value. If there are no 1999 rows, `SUM` over only 1998 rows with `CASE WHEN d_year=1999 THEN ... END` returns NULL because for each row, CASE returns NULL, and SUM of all NULLs is NULL. Yes. So correct.

What if there are 1999 rows with `sales_cnt` sum = 0? Then `curr_cnt` = 0, not NULL. The ratio 0/prev < 0.9 is true, so included. Original: curr_yr row exists with sales_cnt=0, included. Same.

What if there are no 1998 rows? `prev_cnt` = NULL. `prev_cnt > 0` is unknown, excludes. Original: no prev row, join excludes. Same.

What if there are 1998 rows but sum sales_cnt = 0? `prev_cnt` = 0. `prev_cnt > 0` is false, excludes. Original: prev_yr.sales_cnt > 0 false, excludes. Same.

What if there are 1998 rows with sum sales_cnt > 0, and no 1999 rows? `curr_cnt` = NULL, ratio NULL < 0.9 is unknown, excludes. Original: no curr row, join excludes. Same.

So equivalence holds.

Now, let's think about the return join and the fact that we changed `LEFT JOIN` to `JOIN` and moved reason filter to ON. Is it exactly equivalent when there are multiple returns and some don't match reason? Let's test with a simple example.

Sales row S. Returns: R1 reason 7, R2 reason 99.
Original:
S LEFT JOIN Returns ON order/item -> produces (S,R1) and (S,R2). WHERE reason IN (7) -> keeps (S,R1). Result: one row with R1.
Our:
S JOIN Returns ON order/item AND reason IN (7) -> matches only R1. Produces (S,R1). Same.

Sales row S. Returns: R1 reason 99 only.
Original: (S,R1) then WHERE reason IN (7) -> no rows. Our: no match -> no rows. Same.

Sales row S. No returns.
Original: (S,NULL) then WHERE reason IN (7) -> no rows. Our: no match -> no rows. Same.

So equivalent.

Warning: Could not extract SQL candidate from response
Worker 0: Found optimized SQL
ced_suggestion": "Eliminated the self-join on all_sales. Instead of materializing the aggregated CTE and joining it to itself (scanning it twice), a single conditional aggregation (SUM with CASE per year) computes current/previous metrics in one pass. Using SUM without ELSE yields NULL when a year has no rows, exactly matching the original inner-join behavior (missing-year groups are excluded)."
    },
    {
        "group": "Query_optimization",
        "produced_suggestion": "Replaced the comma-style self-join and year arithmetic (1999-1) with a pivot-style aggregation; the output columns prev_year=1998 and year=1999 are constants guaranteed by the filters. UNION (distinct) is intentionally preserved rather than changed to UNION ALL, because deduplication across/within branches is part of the original semantics and changing it would alter results."
    }
]
</advice>

</analysis>
Key rewrites and why they preserve equivalence:

1. Early year filtering: The original all_sales CTE aggregates all years, then the outer self-join discards everything except 1998/1999. Since d_year is part of the deduplicated detail rows, filtering d_year IN (1998,1999) inside each branch cannot affect dedup of the retained rows (year differs, so cross-year rows are never duplicates) and strictly reduces input to the expensive UNION (distinct) and GROUP BY.

2. Self-join elimination: Original groups by (d_year, brand, class, category, manufact), then joins year-1999 rows to year-1998 rows. Conditional aggregation over one group keyed only by item dimensions computes SUM for each year, mathematically identical to pivoting the two grouped rows. Edge cases hold: SUM(CASE...) returns NULL when a year is absent, so NULL > 0 / NULL ratio comparisons exclude the group exactly like a missing join partner; prev_cnt > 0 also guards the division.

3. LEFT JOIN → INNER JOIN: WHERE cr_reason_sk IN (...) rejects NULL join results, so the LEFT JOIN degenerates to an inner join. Moving the reason predicate into the ON clause keeps multi-return multiplication semantics identical (only returns with allowed reasons produce rows) and removes the NULL-extended row generation work.

4. UNION retained: UNION deduplication is part of original semantics; UNION ALL would double-count identical detail rows from different channels, so it is not applied despite being a potential large win.

5. Price ratio division kept as-is (BETWEEN 80*0.01 AND 100*0.01) to guarantee exact equivalence (multiplication rewrite would alter behavior on zero/negative list_price).

Data statistics note: with ~14M catalog_sales, ~29M store_sales, ~7M web_sales and only two years of date_dim (73K rows), pushing d_year down lets the planner use date_dim year filtering/indexing before touching the fact tables, and eliminating the double CTE scan halves the aggregation work.
</analysis>

</enhanced_sql>
```sql
WITH sales_detail AS (
    SELECT d_year, i_brand_id, i_class_id, i_category_id, i_manufact_id,
           cs_quantity - COALESCE(cr_return_quantity, 0) AS sales_cnt,
           cs_ext_sales_price - COALESCE(cr_return_amount, 0.0) AS sales_amt
    FROM catalog_sales
    JOIN item ON i_item_sk = cs_item_sk
    JOIN date_dim ON d_date_sk = cs_sold_date_sk
    JOIN catalog_returns ON cs_order_number = cr_order_number
                        AND cs_item_sk = cr_item_sk
                        AND cr_reason_sk IN (7, 10, 12, 29, 45)
    WHERE i_category = 'Sports'
      AND d_year IN (1998, 1999)
      AND cs_sales_price / cs_list_price BETWEEN 80 * 0.01 AND 100 * 0.01

    UNION

    SELECT d_year, i_brand_id, i_class_id, i_category_id, i_manufact_id,
           ss_quantity - COALESCE(sr_return_quantity, 0),
           ss_ext_sales_price - COALESCE(sr_return_amt, 0.0)
    FROM store_sales
    JOIN item ON i_item_sk = ss_item_sk
    JOIN date_dim ON d_date_sk = ss_sold_date_sk
    JOIN store_returns ON ss_ticket_number = sr_ticket_number
                      AND ss_item_sk = sr_item_sk
                      AND sr_reason_sk IN (7, 10, 12, 29, 45)
    WHERE i_category = 'Sports'
      AND d_year IN (1998, 1999)
      AND ss_sales_price / ss_list_price BETWEEN 80 * 0.01 AND 100 * 0.01

    UNION

    SELECT d_year, i_brand_id, i_class_id, i_category_id, i_manufact_id,
           ws_quantity - COALESCE(wr_return_quantity, 0),
           ws_ext_sales_price - COALESCE(wr_return_amt, 0.0)
    FROM web_sales
    JOIN item ON i_item_sk = ws_item_sk
    JOIN date_dim ON d_date_sk = ws_sold_date_sk
    JOIN web_returns ON ws_order_number = wr_order_number
                    AND ws_item_sk = wr_item_sk
                    AND wr_reason_sk IN (7, 10, 12, 29, 45)
    WHERE i_category = 'Sports'
      AND d_year IN (1998, 1999)
      AND ws_sales_price / ws_list_price BETWEEN 80 * 0.01 AND 100 * 0.01
),
agg AS (
    SELECT i_brand_id, i_class_id, i_category_id, i_manufact_id,
           SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1999 THEN sales_cnt END) AS curr_cnt,
           SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1998 THEN sales_cnt END) AS prev_cnt,
           SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1999 THEN sales_amt END) AS curr_amt,
           SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1998 THEN sales_amt END) AS prev_amt
    FROM sales_detail
    GROUP BY i_brand_id, i_class_id, i_category_id, i_manufact_id
)
SELECT 1998 AS prev_year,
       1999 AS year,
       i_brand_id,
       i_class_id,
       i_category_id,
       i_manufact_id,
       prev_cnt AS prev_yr_cnt,
       curr_cnt AS curr_yr_cnt,
       curr_cnt - prev_cnt AS sales_cnt_diff,
       curr_amt - prev_amt AS sales_amt_diff
FROM agg
WHERE prev_cnt > 0
  AND CAST(curr_cnt AS DECIMAL(17,2)) / CAST(prev_cnt AS DECIMAL(17,2)) < 0.9
ORDER BY sales_cnt_diff, sales_amt_diff
LIMIT 100;
```

The rewrite is sound. The original LEFT JOIN to returns combined with the WHERE reason_sk IN (...) filter effectively behaves as an INNER JOIN, so converting to an explicit INNER JOIN preserves semantics. Pushing d_year IN (1998, 1999) into each branch is safe because the outer self-join only uses years 1999 and 1998 (1999-1). Replacing the self-join on all_sales with conditional aggregation (SUM/CASE pivot) is equivalent: missing-year groups produce NULL sums and are filtered out by the predicate on curr/prev values, matching</analysis>

- LEFT JOIN → INNER JOIN: the predicates `cr_reason_sk IN (...)`, `sr_reason_sk IN (...)`, `wr_reason_sk IN (...)` are null-rejecting, so the original LEFT JOINs were effectively inner joins. The conversion is semantics-preserving; COALESCE is harmlessly retained.
- Conditional aggregation with FILTER replaces the self-join: identical to filtering curr_yr.d_year=1999 / prev_yr.d_year=1998 and joining on brand/class/category/manufact, since the original all_sales aggregation groups by year and the join only combines 1999 with 1998 rows of the same group. Aggregating per year first and then subtracting matches SUM(sales_cnt)/SUM(sales_amt) per (group, year). Also note the original GROUP BY includes d_year; the rewritten aggregates per group with year-conditional sums — mathematically identical.
- Division → multiplication: valid for positive list_price (TPC-DS guarantee); boundary values 0.80/1.00 are inclusive in both forms.
- UNION (not ALL) is preserved, so cross-branch duplicate elimination semantics are unchanged. The HAVING conditions (prev_yr_cnt > 0, ratio < 0.9) match the original WHERE clauses exactly.

=== Start Decision Stage: (Iteration: 0/2) ===
##  2 results, try to select the best one ##
</analysis>
Both rewrites correctly limit to years 1998/1999 early and pivot years into a single aggregation, avoiding the self-join of all_sales. Statement 0 and 1 are nearly identical; the key differences are:

1. Filter placement for reason_sk: Statement 1 moves the return-reason filter into the JOIN condition of catalog_returns/store_returns/web_returns, while Statement 0 places it in WHERE. Semantically these are equivalent for inner joins, so no difference there.

2. Price filter form: Statement 0 rewrites `cs_sales_price / cs_list_price BETWEEN ...` as two range comparisons (`>= 0.80 * cs_list_price AND <= 1.00 * cs_list_price`), avoiding division. However, this changes semantics slightly if cs_list_price can be negative (it can't in practice), and it can enable index/sargable-like comparison pushdown in some engines; it's also numerically safer (avoids division by zero producing NULL and dropping rows, which is actually the same as original behavior... actually division by zero yields NULL/error, whereas the rewritten form would not error). Since the original rows with zero list price are dropped, Statement 0 could include extra rows (list price = 0). That is a semantic risk.

3. UNION vs UNION ALL: Both use UNION (deduplicating) matching the original, which is correct — rows are not unique per key so UNION matters for exact result equivalence.

4. FILTER vs CASE: Statement 0 uses FILTER (Postgres-specific), Statement 1 uses CASE, which is more portable.

Statement 1 keeps the original division-based predicate (preserving exact semantics including NULL-on-zero-list-price behavior), keeps return-reason predicate in join (fine), and uses portable CASE aggregation. Statement 0's price-range rewrite risks changing results when cs_list_price = 0 and relies on FILTER syntax. Therefore Statement 1 is the safest and most effective choice.
</analysis>

    JOIN date_dim ON d_date_sk = ws_sold_date_sk
    JOIN web_returns ON ws_order_number = wr_order_number
                    AND ws_item_sk = wr_item_sk
                    AND wr_reason_sk IN (7, 10, 12, 29, 45)
    WHERE i_category = 'Sports'
      AND d_year IN (1998, 1999)
      AND ws_sales_price / ws_list_price BETWEEN 80 * 0.01 AND 100 * 0.01
),
agg AS (
    SELECT i_brand_id, i_class_id, i_category_id, i_manufact_id,
           SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1999 THEN sales_cnt END) AS curr_cnt,
           SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1998 THEN sales_cnt END) AS prev_cnt,
           SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1999 THEN sales_amt END) AS curr_amt,
           SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1998 THEN sales_amt END) AS prev_amt
    FROM sales_detail
    GROUP BY i_brand_id, i_class_id, i_category_id, i_manufact_id
)
SELECT 1998 AS prev_year,
       1999 AS year,
       i_brand_id,
       i_class_id,
       i_category_id,
       i_manufact_id,
       prev_cnt AS prev_yr_cnt,
       curr_cnt AS curr_yr_cnt,
       curr_cnt - prev_cnt AS sales_cnt_diff,
       curr_amt - prev_amt AS sales_amt_diff
FROM agg
WHERE prev_cnt > 0
  AND CAST(curr_cnt AS DECIMAL(17,2)) / CAST(prev_cnt AS DECIMAL(17,2)) < 0.9
ORDER BY sales_cnt_diff, sales_amt_diff
LIMIT 100; ##
## Rewrite Proposals: [{'group': 'Predicate_simplification', 'produced_suggestion': 'The LEFT JOIN to returns combined with WHERE reason_sk IN (...) effectively behaves as an INNER JOIN (rows without a matching return are filtered out by the NULL reason check). Converting to an explicit INNER JOIN with the reason filter in the ON clause is semantically equivalent and removes dead LEFT JOIN/COALESCE ambiguity. COALESCE on return columns is retained for null-safety.'}, {'group': 'Predicate_simplification', 'produced_suggestion': 'Pushed d_year IN (1998, 1999) into each UNION branch. The outer query only uses years 1999 (current) and 1998 (previous), so filtering early dramatically reduces rows entering the expensive UNION (distinct).'}, {'group': 'Subquery_optimization', 'produced_suggestion': 'Eliminated the self-join on all_sales. Instead of materializing the aggregated CTE and joining it to itself (scanning it twice), a single conditional aggregation (SUM with CASE per year) computes current/previous metrics in one pass. Using SUM without ELSE yields NULL when a year has no rows, exactly matching the original inner-join behavior (missing-year groups are excluded).'}, {'group': 'Query_optimization', 'produced_suggestion': 'Replaced the comma-style self-join and year arithmetic (1999-1) with a pivot-style aggregation; the output columns prev_year=1998 and year=1999 are constants guaranteed by the filters. UNION (distinct) is intentionally preserved rather than changed to UNION ALL, because deduplication across/within branches is part of the original semantics and changing it would alter results.'}] ##

The original query uses a CTE (`all_sales`) that is materialized once and then scanned twice (as `curr_yr` and `prev_yr`) via a self-join with a year filter applied at the CTE-scan level. The rewritten query eliminates the CTE entirely: it folds the year-dependent logic into a single aggregation pass using conditional aggregation (CASE WHEN pivoting of d_year), and the year filter is pushed down into the `date_dim` index scans (`Filter: d_year = ANY ('{1998,1999}')`). The enhanced plan is structurally and cost-wise identical to the rewritten plan — the rewriting already captured all available improvements, so no further optimization (e.g., additional index usage, join reordering, or parallelism changes) was applied by the enhancer.

Key observations:
- The planner-reported cost is essentially unchanged (~514,997.82 → 514,997.82) because the expensive part (the parallel scan of store/catalog/web returns joined with sales and item) dominates and is untouched in both versions. However, the true runtime benefit lies in avoiding CTE materialization and the double CTE scan, which the cost model does not fully reflect.
- The most expensive node (the Parallel Append / Nested Loop over store_returns at ~326,426) is unchanged; the optimization targeted the post-aggregation layer, not the scan layer.
- The rewritten plan adds a year filter to all three date_dim Index Scans, reducing rows flowing upward into the aggregation.
- Worker count (2 workers, Parallel Append with three branches) and all join algorithms (Parallel Hash Join + Nested Loop) are preserved.

</analysis>

1. Cost Efficiency
- Overall cost change: essentially flat at the planner level — from 514,997.82 (original) to 514,997.82 (rewritten and enhanced), a change of ~0%. This is because the dominant cost comes from the large parallel scans/joins over store_returns, catalog_returns, and web_returns, which are identical across all three plans.
- Most expensive node: the store_returns Nested Loop branch (~326,426.08) is unchanged. However, the actual execution efficiency improves in the rewritten/enhanced plans because the CTE materialization and the two CTE scans (curr_yr/prev_yr self-join) are replaced by a single aggregation pass — a real-workload saving that the cost model under-represents.
- Additional filtering benefit: the rewritten plan pushes `d_year = ANY ('{1998,1999}')` down into all three date_dim Index Scan nodes, pruning non-target years at the dimension-table level rather than at the CTE-scan stage, reducing intermediate rows fed into aggregation.

2. Plan Characteristics
- Scan type transitions: none at the leaf level — Parallel Seq Scans on the returns tables, Bitmap Index Scans on item (via idx_item_category), and Index Scans on the sales fact tables and date_dim are retained, as they were already optimal. The improvement is logical, not physical-access based.
- Join algorithm refinements: no algorithm changes (Parallel Hash Join + Nested Loop preserved), but the explicit CTE self-join (curr_yr ⋈ prev_yr with a five-condition join filter including a ratio predicate) is eliminated entirely, replaced by conditional aggregation with a HAVING-style filter on the GroupAggregate node. This removes a potentially expensive cartesian-style self-join over the aggregated CTE.
- Elimination of sorting/intermediate results: the original CTE would be materialized and scanned twice; the rewritten plan computes the aggregation in one pass with CASE-based pivoting, avoiding the extra CTE Scan pipeline. The sort/unique structure beneath the aggregation remains (required for deduplication), but the CTE materialization overhead and the join-filter-based year matching disappear.

3. Resource Utilization
- Memory: the original plan implicitly requires materializing the all_sales CTE (or memoizing it for two scans); the rewritten plan removes this intermediate result set entirely, lowering memory/spill pressure. The three Parallel Hash nodes (one per channel, each building the ~4,540-row Sports item hash) are unchanged in size and count.
- Workers: unchanged — 2 parallel workers with a 3-branch Parallel Append in all versions; parallelism was already well exploited in the original.

4. Other Improvements
- Single-pass aggregation: year-over-year comparison is now performed inside the GroupAggregate via CASE WHEN sums plus an aggregate-level filter, eliminating the post-hoc CTE join and its numeric-cast join condition — cleaner, more robust, and less error-prone.
- Predicate pushdown: year filtering moved from the outer CTE scans into the date_dim index-scan level, improving selectivity propagation.
- Enhanced plan = rewritten plan: the enhancement stage produced no further changes, indicating the rewrite reached a stable local optimum for this query given the existing indexes and data distribution; remaining cost is bound by the returns-table scans, where further gains would require new indexing or partitioning strategies rather than query rewriting.

Thus rewrite is equivalent.

Performance improvements:
- Original all_sales CTE materializes aggregated result for all years, then self-joins. Our rewrite filters years early and aggregates once, no self-join. This reduces work.
- The conditional aggregation computes both years in one pass per dimension group. It avoids scanning CTE twice and joining.
- Pushing d_year into branches reduces the number of sales/returns/date rows processed. Especially date_dim has many years; original aggregated all years then filtered. This is significant.
- Keeping LEFT JOIN with WHERE reason: The planner already treats as inner join, so no performance loss. If we wanted, we could convert to INNER JOIN with reason in ON, but not necessary. The previous report's concern about semantics is resolved by keeping original join form.
- Index suggestions: item(i_category), returns(reason_sk), sales(item_sk/date), date_dim(d_year). But the explain shows item idx used. The reason filter on returns uses seq scan because many returns. With reason in list, maybe index on reason_sk could help? Explain shows parallel seq scan on returns with filter reason. There are 2.1M catalog_returns, 7.2M store_returns, 1.4M web_returns. Reason filter selects 5 of 45 reasons, ~11%. Seq scan may be okay. If performance still poor, consider index on returns(reason_sk, item_sk, order_number/ticket). But query rewriting is main. We can mention optional indexes but not required.
- Also date_dim filter d_year IN (1998,1999) might allow index on date_dim(d_year) if date_dim joined by d_date_sk. But date_dim is small (73k rows), filter can be applied. Could filter date_dim first. In our SQL

WITH sales_detail AS (
    SELECT d_year,
           i_brand_id,
           i_class_id,
           i_category_id,
           i_manufact_id,
           cs_quantity - COALESCE(cr_return_quantity, 0) AS sales_cnt,
           cs_ext_sales_price - COALESCE(cr_return_amount, 0.0) AS sales_amt
    FROM catalog_sales
    JOIN item ON i_item_sk = cs_item_sk
    JOIN date_dim ON d_date_sk = cs_sold_date_sk
    JOIN catalog_returns ON cs_order_number = cr_order_number
                        AND cs_item_sk = cr_item_sk
                        AND cr_reason_sk IN (7, 10, 12, 29, 45)
    WHERE i_category = 'Sports'
      AND d_year IN (1998, 1999)
      AND cs_sales_price / cs_list_price BETWEEN 0.80 AND 1.00

    UNION

    SELECT d_year,
           i_brand_id,
           i_class_id,
           i_category_id,
           i_manufact_id,
           ss_quantity - COALESCE(sr_return_quantity, 0),
           ss_ext_sales_price - COALESCE(sr_return_amt, 0.0)
    FROM store_sales
    JOIN item ON i_item_sk = ss_item_sk
    JOIN date_dim ON d_date_sk = ss_sold_date_sk
    JOIN store_returns ON ss_ticket_number = sr_ticket_number
                      AND ss_item_sk = sr_item_sk
                      AND sr_reason_sk IN (7, 10, 12, 29, 45)
    WHERE i_category = 'Sports'
      AND d_year IN (1998, 1999)
      AND ss_sales_price / ss_list_price BETWEEN 0.80 AND 1.00

    UNION

    SELECT d_year,
           i_brand_id,
           i_class_id,
           i_category_id,
           i_manufact_id,
           ws_quantity - COALESCE(wr_return_quantity, 0),
           ws_ext_sales_price - COALESCE(wr_return_amt, 0.0)
    FROM web_sales
    JOIN item ON i_item_sk = ws_item_sk
    JOIN date_dim ON d_date_sk = ws_sold_date_sk
    JOIN web_returns ON ws_order_number = wr_order_number
                    AND ws_item_sk = wr_item_sk
                    AND wr_reason_sk IN (7, 10, 12, 29,

</analysis>
The original query materializes all_sales over every year, then scans it twice in a self-join restricted to 1999 and 1998. Since the outer query only consumes those two years, filtering d_year IN (1998, 1999) inside each UNION branch is equivalent and reduces scanned rows significantly (date_dim: 73049 rows; sales tables total ~50M rows, so reducing years helps). The self-join is replaced by one GROUP BY with conditional aggregation, computing both years in a single pass. The NULL-rejection behavior of the original WHERE reason_sk predicates is preserved by keeping the LEFT JOINs and predicates in WHERE, maintaining exact equivalence while allowing the planner to optimize join order as it already does.
</analysis>

WITH sales_detail AS (
    SELECT
        d_year,
        i_brand_id,
        i_class_id,
        i_category_id,
        i_manufact_id,
        cs_quantity - COALESCE(cr_return_quantity, 0) AS sales_cnt,
        cs_ext_sales_price - COALESCE(cr_return_amount, 0.0) AS sales_amt
    FROM catalog_sales
    JOIN item ON i_item_sk = cs_item_sk
    JOIN date_dim ON d_date_sk = cs_sold_date_sk
    LEFT JOIN catalog_returns
           ON cs_order_number = cr_order_number
          AND cs_item_sk = cr_item_sk
    WHERE i_category = 'Sports'
      AND d_year IN (1998, 1999)
      AND cs_sales_price / cs_list_price BETWEEN 0.80 AND 1.00
      AND cr_reason_sk IN (7, 10, 12, 29, 45)

    UNION

    SELECT
        d_year,
        i_brand_id,
        i_class_id,
        i_category_id,
        i_manufact_id,
        ss_quantity - COALESCE(sr_return_quantity, 0),
        ss_ext_sales_price - COALESCE(sr_return_amt, 0.0)
    FROM store_sales
    JOIN item ON i_item_sk = ss_item_sk
    JOIN date_dim ON d_date_sk = ss_sold_date_sk
    LEFT JOIN store_returns
           ON ss_ticket_number = sr_ticket_number
          AND ss_item_sk = sr_item_sk
    WHERE i_category = 'Sports'
      AND d_year IN (1998, 1999)
      AND ss_sales_price / ss_list_price BETWEEN 0.80 AND 1.00
      AND sr_reason_sk IN (7, 10, 12, 29, 45)

    UNION

    SELECT
        d_year,
        i_brand_id,
        i_class_id,
        i_category_id,
        i_manufact_id,
        ws_quantity - COALESCE(wr_return_quantity, 0),
        ws_ext_sales_price - COALESCE(wr_return_amt, 0.0)
    FROM web_sales
    JOIN item ON i_item_sk = ws_item_sk
    JOIN date_dim ON d_date_sk = ws_sold_date_sk
    LEFT JOIN web_returns
           ON ws_order_number = wr_order_number
          AND ws_item_sk = wr_item_sk
    WHERE i_category = 'Sports'
      AND d_year IN (1998, 1999)
      AND ws_sales_price / ws_list_price BETWEEN 0.80 AND 1.00
      AND wr_reason_sk IN (7, 10, 12, 29, 45)
),
agg AS (
    SELECT
        i_brand_id,
        i_class_id,
        i_category_id,
        i_manufact_id,
        SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1999 THEN sales_cnt END) AS curr_cnt,
        SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1998 THEN sales_cnt END) AS prev_cnt,
        SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1999 THEN sales_amt END) AS curr_amt,
        SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1998 THEN sales_amt END) AS prev_amt
    FROM sales_detail
    GROUP BY i_brand_id, i_class_id, i_category_id, i_manufact_id
)
SELECT
    1998 AS prev_year,
    1999 AS year,
    i_brand_id,
    i_class_id,
    i_category_id,
    i_manufact_id,
    prev_cnt AS prev_yr_cnt,
    curr_cnt AS curr_yr_cnt,
    curr_cnt - prev_cnt AS sales_cnt_diff,
    curr_amt - prev_amt AS sales_amt_diff
FROM agg
WHERE prev_cnt > 0
  AND CAST(curr_cnt AS DECIMAL(17,2)) / CAST(prev_cnt AS DECIMAL(17,2)) < 0.9
ORDER BY sales_cnt_diff, sales_amt_diff
LIMIT 100;

</analysis>
Option 0 changes the LEFT JOIN to an INNER JOIN on the returns tables, which alters semantics (sales rows without matching returns would be dropped), even though it also pushes the reason_sk filter into the join. Option 1 preserves the original LEFT JOIN semantics with COALESCE handling, keeps the reason_sk filter in the WHERE clause (equivalent to the original), adds a safe d_year IN (1998, 1999) pushdown that reduces scanned data since only those years are used, and replaces the costly self-join of all_sales with a conditional-aggregation pivot — a significant performance improvement. Therefore option 1 is the best.
</analysis>

## selected Worker 1##
## Selected Rewritten SQL: WITH sales_detail AS (
    SELECT
        d_year,
        i_brand_id,
        i_class_id,
        i_category_id,
        i_manufact_id,
        cs_quantity - COALESCE(cr_return_quantity, 0) AS sales_cnt,
        cs_ext_sales_price - COALESCE(cr_return_amount, 0.0) AS sales_amt
    FROM catalog_sales
    JOIN item ON i_item_sk = cs_item_sk
    JOIN date_dim ON d_date_sk = cs_sold_date_sk
    LEFT JOIN catalog_returns
           ON cs_order_number = cr_order_number
          AND cs_item_sk = cr_item_sk
    WHERE i_category = 'Sports'
      AND d_year IN (1998, 1999)
      AND cs_sales_price / cs_list_price BETWEEN 0.80 AND 1.00
      AND cr_reason_sk IN (7, 10, 12, 29, 45)

    UNION

    SELECT
        d_year,
        i_brand_id,
        i_class_id,
        i_category_id,
        i_manufact_id,
        ss_quantity - COALESCE(sr_return_quantity, 0),
        ss_ext_sales_price - COALESCE(sr_return_amt, 0.0)
    FROM store_sales
    JOIN item ON i_item_sk = ss_item_sk
    JOIN date_dim ON d_date_sk = ss_sold_date_sk
    LEFT JOIN store_returns
           ON ss_ticket_number = sr_ticket_number
          AND ss_item_sk = sr_item_sk
    WHERE i_category = 'Sports'
      AND d_year IN (1998, 1999)
      AND ss_sales_price / ss_list_price BETWEEN 0.80 AND 1.00
      AND sr_reason_sk IN (7, 10, 12, 29, 45)

    UNION

    SELECT
        d_year,
        i_brand_id,
        i_class_id,
        i_category_id,
        i_manufact_id,
        ws_quantity - COALESCE(wr_return_quantity, 0),
        ws_ext_sales_price - COALESCE(wr_return_amt, 0.0)
    FROM web_sales
    JOIN item ON i_item_sk = ws_item_sk
    JOIN date_dim ON d_date_sk = ws_sold_date_sk
    LEFT JOIN web_returns
           ON ws_order_number = wr_order_number
          AND ws_item_sk = wr_item_sk
    WHERE i_category = 'Sports'
      AND d_year IN (1998, 1999)
      AND ws_sales_price / ws_list_price BETWEEN 0.80 AND 1.00
      AND wr_reason_sk IN (7, 10, 12, 29, 45)
),
agg AS (
    SELECT
        i_brand_id,
        i_class_id,
        i_category_id,
        i_manufact_id,
        SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1999 THEN sales_cnt END) AS curr_cnt,
        SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1998 THEN sales_cnt END) AS prev_cnt,
        SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1999 THEN sales_amt END) AS curr_amt,
        SUM(CASE WHEN d_year = 1998 THEN sales_amt END) AS prev_amt
    FROM sales_detail
    GROUP BY i_brand_id, i_class_id, i_category_id, i_manufact_id
)
SELECT
    1998 AS prev_year,
    1999 AS year,
    i_brand_id,
    i_class_id,
    i_category_id,
    i_manufact_id,
    prev_cnt AS prev_yr_cnt,
    curr_cnt AS curr_yr_cnt,
    curr_cnt - prev_cnt AS sales_cnt_diff,
    curr_amt - prev_amt AS sales_amt_diff
FROM agg
WHERE prev_cnt > 0
  AND CAST(curr_cnt AS DECIMAL(17,2)) / CAST(prev_cnt AS DECIMAL(17,2)) < 0.9
ORDER BY sales_cnt_diff, sales_amt_diff
LIMIT 100; ##

</analysis>
Comparing the three plans, the original query materializes a CTE (all_sales) and then scans it twice (curr_yr / prev_yr), joining the two CTE scans with a Nested Loop that carries a five-condition Join Filter plus a ratio predicate. The rewritten and enhanced plans are structurally identical to each other: they replace the CTE self-join with a single-pass conditional aggregation (CASE WHEN inside SUM) over a subquery (sales_detail → agg), and they push the year filter (d_year IN (1998, 1999)) down into the date_dim index scans.

Cost-wise, the planner's estimates are essentially unchanged: the top-level Limit cost stays at 514997.82..514997.83 in all three plans, and the most expensive node (the Gather feeding the Parallel Append, itself dominated by the Parallel Seq Scan on store_returns at 167793.77) moves only from 514997.45 to 514997.47 — a difference of well under 0.001%. This is because the planner estimates the CTE at only ~3 rows, so the estimated cost of the CTE self-join is trivially small. The real benefit of the rewrite is structural and would show up in actual runtime: one pass over the detail data instead of materializing and scanning it twice, and early elimination of non-target years at the date_dim index scan level rather than filtering CTE output afterward.

Plan-shape changes between original and rewritten/enhanced:
- The CTE node disappears entirely; two CTE Scan nodes and the Nested Loop that joins them (with its expensive Join Filter over brand/class/category/manufacturer plus a numeric division predicate) are removed.
- The GroupAggregate moves below the final sort and gains an aggregate Filter implementing the "prior-year count > 0" and "ratio < 0.9" conditions directly on aggregated values, so rows that fail the ratio check are rejected before the outer sort/limit rather than after a join.
- Each date_dim Index Scan gains a Filter (d_year = ANY ('{1998,1999}')), restricting rows at the earliest possible point.
- No change to scan types (Parallel Seq Scan on the returns tables, Parallel Bitmap Heap Scan + Bitmap Index Scan on item, Index Scans on the fact-table primary keys), no change to join algorithms (Parallel Hash Join + Nested Loop structure preserved), and no change in worker count (2 workers).

Resource utilization: the Parallel Hash nodes and their build inputs are identical across all three plans, so hash memory usage is unchanged. Memory is saved indirectly by removing the CTE materialization (tuplestore) and the intermediate result set of the CTE self-join. The rewritten and enhanced plans are byte-for-byte equivalent in structure and cost, indicating the enhancement did not alter the physical plan beyond what the rewrite achieved — the remaining bottleneck (Parallel Seq Scan on store_returns, ~33% of total cost) is untouched and represents the next optimization opportunity.
</analysis>

</report>
1. Cost Efficiency
- Overall cost change: effectively 0%. The top-level cost is 514997.82..514997.83 in the original, rewritten, and enhanced plans. The rewrite does not reduce the planner's estimated cost because the CTE is estimated at only ~3 rows, making the eliminated CTE self-join appear nearly free to the optimizer. Actual runtime, however, should improve since the plan now reads the detail data once instead of materializing a CTE and scanning it twice.
- Cost at the most expensive plan node: the dominant node (Gather over the Parallel Append, ultimately driven by the Parallel Seq Scan on store_returns) shifts marginally from 514997.45 to 514997.47 in the rewritten/enhanced plans — a negligible (<0.001%) change. The store_returns scan (cost ≈167793.77) remains the single largest cost contributor and is untouched by the rewrite.

2. Plan Characteristics
- Scan type transitions: none. All scans keep the same access methods (Parallel Seq Scan on store_returns/catalog_returns/web_returns, Parallel Bitmap Heap Scan with Bitmap Index Scan on item via idx_item_category, and Index Scans on the fact-table primary keys and idx_date_dim_sk). However, each date_dim Index Scan in the rewritten/enhanced plans gains a pushed-down Filter (d_year = ANY ('{1998,1999}')), applying the year restriction at the source instead of after aggregation.
- Join algorithm refinements: the parallel hash joins and nested loops over the base tables are unchanged. The significant refinement is at the logical level: the original's Nested Loop joining two CTE scans (with a 5-condition Join Filter plus a numeric ratio predicate) is eliminated entirely, replaced by a single GroupAggregate with conditional SUM(CASE WHEN ...) expressions — converting a self-join into a one-pass aggregation.
- Sorting/intermediate results: the number of Sort nodes above the data-gathering stage decreases (the original had a final Sort fed by a CTE self-join; the rewritten plan sorts the aggregated output directly). The CTE materialization — a full intermediate result set — is removed, and the aggregate-level Filter (prev_cnt > 0 and ratio < 0.9) prunes rows before the final sort/limit rather than after a join, reducing the intermediate result set flowing to the top of the plan.

3. Resource Utilization
- Memory usage: the Parallel Hash builds for the item dimension are identical across all three plans, so hash memory consumption is unchanged. The main memory win is the removal of the CTE tuplestore and the intermediate rows produced by the CTE self-join; in the rewritten plans all grouping is done streaming through Sort + GroupAggregate without a materialization boundary.
- Worker count: unchanged at 2 workers (Gather with Workers Planned: 2, Parallel Append over three channel branches) in every plan. Parallelism strategy is preserved.

4. Other Improvements
- Elimination of the CTE: the rewritten plans avoid the optimization fence and double-evaluation inherent to the materialized all_sales CTE, replacing the curr/prev self-join with single-pass conditional aggregation. This removes a join whose cost grows with the number of brand/class/category/manufacturer combinations.
- Earlier predicate pushdown: the d_year IN (1998, 1999) filter is applied inside every date_dim Index Scan, so irrelevant years are discarded before rows ever reach the aggregation, and the ratio/rule predicates are applied as an aggregate Filter, avoiding post-join evaluation.
- Rewritten vs. enhanced plans: the enhanced EXPLAIN is identical in structure, node types, and cost to the rewritten plan; the enhancement produced no further physical-plan changes. The remaining optimization target is the Parallel Seq Scan on store_returns (~33% of total cost, filtered on sr_reason_sk), which could benefit from an index on sr_reason_sk if this workload is frequent.

</analysis>
Comparing the three plans, the original query materializes a CTE (all_sales) and then scans it twice (curr_yr / prev_yr), joining the two CTE scans with a Nested Loop that carries a five-condition Join Filter plus a ratio predicate. The rewritten and enhanced plans are structurally identical to each other: they replace the CTE self-join with a single-pass conditional aggregation (CASE WHEN inside SUM) over a subquery (sales_detail → agg), and they push the year filter (d_year IN (1998, 1999)) down into the date_dim index scans.

Cost-wise, the planner's estimates are essentially unchanged: the top-level Limit cost stays at 514997.82..514997.83 in all three plans, and the most expensive node (the Gather feeding the Parallel Append, itself dominated by the Parallel Seq Scan on store_returns at 167793.77) moves only from 514997.45 to 514997.47 — a difference of well under 0.001%. This is because the planner estimates the CTE at only ~3 rows, so the estimated cost of the CTE self-join is trivially small. The real benefit of the rewrite is structural and would show up in actual runtime: one pass over the detail data instead of materializing and scanning it twice, and early elimination of non-target years at the date_dim index scan level rather than filtering CTE output afterward.

Plan-shape changes between original and rewritten/enhanced:
- The CTE node disappears entirely; two CTE Scan nodes and the Nested Loop that joins them (with its expensive Join Filter over brand/class/category/manufacturer plus a numeric division predicate) are removed.
- The GroupAggregate moves below the final sort and gains an aggregate Filter implementing the "prior-year count > 0" and "ratio < 0.9" conditions directly on aggregated values, so rows that fail the ratio check are rejected before the outer sort/limit rather than after a join.
- Each date_dim Index Scan gains a Filter (d_year = ANY ('{1998,1999}')), restricting rows at the earliest possible point.
- No change to scan types (Parallel Seq Scan on the returns tables, Parallel Bitmap Heap Scan + Bitmap Index Scan on item, Index Scans on the fact-table primary keys), no change to join algorithms (Parallel Hash Join + Nested Loop structure preserved), and no change in worker count (2 workers).
